53:B2 | 71:Enrollment by Racial/Ethnic Category.
71:Provide numbers of undergraduate students for each of the following categories as of the institution’s official fall reporting date or as of | 303:October 15, 2024 | 333:.
71:• | 78:Include international students only in the category "Nonresidents."
71:• | 78:Complete the “Total Undergraduates” column only if you cannot provide data for the first two columns.
71:• | 78:Report as your institution reports to IPEDS: persons who are Hispanic should be reported only on the Hispanic line, not under any race, and persons who are
78:non-Hispanic multi-racial should be reported only under "Two or more races."
71:• | 78:New guidance from IPEDS for reporting aggregate data: Racial/ethnic designations are requested only for United States citizens, residents, and other eligible
78:non-citizens.
71:Eligible non-citizens include all students who completed high school or a GED equivalency within the United States
71:(including DACA and undocumented students) and who were not on an F-1 non-immigrant student visa at the time of
71:high school graduation.
71:More information about other eligible (for financial aid purposes) non-citizens is available at
71:https://studentaid.gov/understand-aid/eligibility/requirements/non-us-citizens
71:Nonresident - A person who is not a citizen or national of the United States and who is in this country on a student visa
71:or temporary basis and does not have the right to remain indefinitely. Do not include DACA, undocumented, or other
71:eligible noncitizens in this category.
71:NOTE - Nonresidents are to be reported separately, in the boxes provided, rather than included in any of the seven
71:racial/ethnic categories or in race/ethnicity unknown.
71:Dual Enrollment: If your institution enrolls high school students in college courses for credit either | 238:within a dual enrollment program | 298:or | 303:outside of a dual enrollment program, | 370:you may report the unduplicated count as part of the non-degree-
71:seeking students included in the “Total Undergraduates (both degree & non-degree-seeking)” section.

53:B22 | 71:B22. Retention Rates
71:Report for the cohort of all full-time, first-time bachelor’s (or equivalent) degree-seeking undergraduate
71:students who entered in Fall 2023 (or the preceding summer term).
71:• The initial cohort may be adjusted for students who departed for the following reasons:
71:* Death
71:* Permanent Disability
71:* Service in the armed forces
71:* Foreign aid service of the federal government
71:* Official church missions
175:Percent
70:For the cohort of all full-time bachelor’s (or equivalent)
70:degree-seeking undergraduate students who entered
70:your institution as first-year students in Fall 2023 (or the
70:preceding summer term), what percentage was enrolled
70:at your institution as of the date your institution
51:B2201 | 70:calculates its official enrollment in Fall 2024. | 184:64.6 Enrollment And Persistence | 248:Retention Rates | 321:Degree-seeking | 372:Undergraduates | 393:First-time first-year | 413:All | 433:FT | 453:All | 474:Whole Number or Round to Nearest Tenths
71:Total students retained = students from the Fall 2023 cohort who are still enrolled as of Fall 2024 +
71:students from Fall 2023 cohort who completed their bachelor’s program as of Fall 2024
71:(Students from the Fall 2023 cohort still enrolled as of Fall 2024 + Students from Fall 2023 cohort who
71:completed their bachelor’s program as of Fall 2024)/(Adjusted Fall 2023 cohort) *100
71:Note: The number of first-time students seeking a bachelor’s degree (or equivalent) who attain a
71:bachelor’s degree (or equivalent) by their second fall term is expected to be zero or very small. In
71:exceptional cases when a first-time student does satisfy all degree requirements including full credit
71:completion (e.g., typically 120 credit hours) and is awarded a bachelor’s degree (or equivalent) by their
71:second fall term, they are to be considered “retained” for EF reporting purposes.

276:H. FINANCIAL AID
80:Please refer to the following financial aid definitions when completing Section H.
80:Awarded aid: | 111:The dollar amounts offered to financial aid applicants
80:Financial aid applicant: | 133:Any applicant who submits any one of the institutionally required financial aid applications/forms, such as the FAFSA.
80:Indebtedness: | 113:Aggregate dollar amount borrowed through any loan program (federal, state, subsidized, unsubsidized, private, etc.; excluding parent loans) while the student was enrolled at an
80:institution. Student loans co-signed by a parent are assumed to be the responsibility of the student and | 308:should | 324:be included.
80:Institutional scholarships and grants: | 164:Endowed scholarships, annual gifts and tuition funded grants for which the institution determines the recipient.
80:Financial need: | 115:As determined by your institution using the federal methodology and/or your institution's own standards.
80:Need-based aid: | 118:College-funded or college-administered award from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify. This includes both
80:institutional and non-institutional student aid (grants, jobs, and loans).
80:Need-based scholarship or grant aid: | 164:Scholarships and grants from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify.
80:Need-based self-help aid: | 138:Loans and jobs from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must demonstrate financial need to qualify.
80:Non-need-based scholarship or grant aid: | 174:Scholarships and grants, gifts, or merit-based aid from institutional, state, federal, or other sources (including unrestricted funds or gifts and endowment
80:income) awarded solely on the basis of academic achievement, merit, or any other non-need-based reason. When reporting questions H1 and H2, non-need-based aid that is used to meet need
80:should be counted as need-based aid.
80:Note: Suggested order of precedence for counting non-need money as need-based:
80:1. Non-need institutional grants | 186:6. Non-need outside grants
80:2. Non-need tuition waivers | 186:7. Non-need student loans
80:3. Non-need athletic awards | 186:8. Non-need parent loans
80:4. Non-need federal grants | 186:9. Non-need work
80:5. Non-need state grants
80:Non-need-based self-help aid: | 149:Loans and jobs from institutional, state, or other sources for which a student need not demonstrate financial need to qualify.
80:Private student loans: | 131:A nonfederal loan made by a lender such as a bank, credit union or private lender used to pay for up to the annual cost of education, less any financial aid received.
80:External scholarships and grants: | 155:Scholarships and grants received from outside (private) sources that students bring with them (e.g., Kiwanis, National Merit scholarships). The institution may
80:process paperwork to receive the dollars, but it has no role in determining the recipient or the dollar amount awarded.
80:Work study and employment: | 148:Federal and state work study aid, and any employment packaged by your institution in financial aid awards.
80:Aid Awarded to Enrolled Undergraduates
54:H1
80:Enter total dollar amounts | 139:awarded | 160:to enrolled full-time and less than full-time degree-seeking undergraduates | 326:(using the same cohort reported in CDS Question B1, “total degree-seeking”
80:undergraduates) | 118:in the following categories.
80:• | 89:If the data being reported are final figures for the 2023-2024 academic year (see the next item below),
87:use the 2023-2024 academic year's CDS Question B1 cohort.
80:• | 89:Include aid awarded to international students (i.e., those not qualifying for federal aid).
80:• | 89:Aid that is non-need-based but that was used to meet need should be reported in the need-based aid
87:column.
80:• | 89:For a suggested order of precedence in assigning categories of aid to cover need, see the entry for “non-
87:need-based scholarship or grant aid” on the last page of the definitions section.

54:H5 | 80:H5. Number and percent of students in class (defined in H4 above) borrowing from federal, non-federal, and any loan sources, and the average (or mean) amount borrowed.
80:• | 89:The “Average per-undergraduate-borrower cumulative principal borrowed,” is designed to provide better
87:information about student borrowing from federal and nonfederal (institutional, state, commercial) sources.

87:the particular row. For example, the federal loans average (row b) should only be the cumulative average of
87:federal loans and the private loans average (row e) should only be the cumulative average of private loans.

53:I | 55:- | 56:3 | 76:Undergraduate Class Size
76:In the table below, please use the following definitions to report information about the size of classes and class sections offered in the Fall 2024 term.
76:• Do not count undergraduate or graduate student teaching assistants as faculty.
76:Class Sections: | 107:A class section is an organized course offered for credit, identified by discipline and number, meeting at a stated time or times in a classroom or similar setting, and not a subsection such as a laboratory or
76:discussion session. Undergraduate class sections are defined as any sections in which at least one degree-seeking undergraduate student is enrolled for credit. Exclude distance learning classes and noncredit classes and
76:individual instruction such as dissertation or thesis research, music instruction, or one-to-one readings. Exclude students in independent study, co-operative programs, internships, foreign language taped tutor sessions,
76:practicums, and all students in one-on-one classes. Each class section should be counted only once and should not be duplicated because of course catalog cross-listings.
76:Class Subsections: | 114:A class subsection includes any subsection of a course, such as laboratory, recitation, and discussion subsections that are supplementary in nature and are scheduled to meet separately from the lecture
76:portion of the course. Undergraduate subsections are defined as any subsections of courses in which degree-seeking undergraduate students enrolled for credit. As above, exclude noncredit classes and individual
76:instruction such as dissertation or thesis research, music instruction, or one-to-one readings. Each class subsection should be counted only once and should not be duplicated because of cross-listings.
76:Using the above definitions, please report for each of the following class-size intervals the number of | 276:class sections | 303:and | 311:class subsections | 346:offered in Fall 2024. For example, a lecture class with 800 students who met at
76:another time in 40 separate labs with 20 students should be counted once in the “100+” column in the class section column and 40 times under the “20-29” column of the class subsections table.

51:*Academic advisement: | 51:Accelerated program: | 64:Completion of a college program of study in fewer than the usual number of years, most often by attending summer sessions and carrying extra courses during the regular academic term | 66:Plan under which each student is assigned to a faculty member or a trained adviser, who, through regular meetings, helps the student plan and implement immediate and long-term academic and vocational goals. | 170:.
51:Admitted student: | 62:Applicant who is offered admission to a degree-granting program | 100:at your institution.
51:*Adult student services: | 51:American Indian or Alaska Native: | 66:Admission assistance, support, orientation, and other services expressly for adults who have started college for the first time, or who are re-entering after a lapse of a few years. | 73:A person having origins in any of the original peoples of North and South America (including Central America) and maintaining tribal affiliation or community attachment.
51:Applicant (first-time, first year): | 51:Application fee: | 51:Asian: | 55:A person having origins in any of the original peoples of the Far East, Southeast Asia, or the Indian subcontinent, including, for example, Cambodia, China, India, Japan, Korea, M alaysia, Pakistan, the Philippine Islands, Thailand, and Vietnam. | 61:That amount of money that an institution charges for processing a student’s application for acceptance. This amount is | 71:An individual who has fulfilled the institution’s requirements to be considered for admission (including payment or waiving of the application fee, if any) and who has been notified of one of the following actions: admission, nonadmission, placement on waiting list, or application withdrawn (by applicant or institution). | 130:not | 132:creditable toward tuition and required fees, nor is it refundable if the student is not admitted to the institution.
51:Associate degree: | 51:Bachelor’s degree: | 62:An award that normally requires at least two but less than four years of full-time equivalent college work. | 63:An award (baccalaureate or equivalent degree, as determined by the Secretary of the U.S. Department of Education) that normally requires at least four years but | 156:not | 158:more than five years of full-time equivalent college-level work. This includes ALL bachelor’s degrees conferred in a five-year cooperative (work-study plan) program. (A cooperative plan provides for alternate class attendance and employment in business, industry, or government; thus, it allows students to combine actual work experience with their college studies.) Also, it includes bachelor’s degrees in which the normal four years of work are completed in three years.
51:Black or African American | 51:Board (charges): | 61:Assume average cost for 19 meals per week or the maximum meal plan. | 67:: | 69:A person having origins in any of the black racial groups of Africa.
51:Books and supplies (costs): | 51:Calendar system: | 62:The method by which an institution structures most of its courses for the academic year. | 68:Average cost of books and supplies. Do not include unusual costs for special groups of students (e.g., engineering or art majors), unless they constitute the majority of students at your institution.
51:Campus Ministry: | 63:Religious student organizations (denominational or nondenominational) devoted to fostering religious life on college campuses. M ay also refer to Campus Crusade for Christ, an interdenominational Christian organization.
51:*Career and placement services: | 51:Carnegie units: | 51:Certificate: | 58:See | 61:Postsecondary award, certificate, or diploma. | 61:One year of study or the equivalent in a secondary school subject. | 71:A range of services, including (often) the following: coordination of visits of employers to campus; aptitude and vocational testing; interest inventories, personal counseling; help in resume writing, interviewing, launching the job search; listings for those students desiring employment and those seeking permanent positions; establishment of a permanent reference folder; career resource materials.
51:Class rank: | 51:College-preparatory program: | 58:The relative numerical position of a student in his or her graduating class, calculated by the high school on the basis of grade-point average, whether weighted or unweighted. | 70:Courses in academic subjects (English, history and social studies, foreign languages, mathematics, science, and the arts) that stress preparation for college or university study.
51:Common Application: | 51:*Community service program: | 65:The standard application form distributed by the National Association of Secondary School Principals for a large number of private colleges who are members of the Common Application Group. | 70:Referral center for students wishing to perform volunteer work in the community or participate in volunteer activities coordinated by academic departments.
51:Commuter: | 51:Comprehensive transition and postsecondary program for students with intellectual disabilities: | 58:A student who lives off campus in housing that is not owned by, operated by, or affiliated with the college. This category includes students who commute from home and students who have moved to the area to attend college. | 111:Programs designed to support postsecondary students with intellectual disabilities obtain instruction in academic, career and technical, and independent living subjects in preparation for employment.
51:Clock hour: | 51:Continuous basis (for program enrollment): | 59:A unit of measure that represents an hour of scheduled instruction given to students. Also referred to as contact hour. | 79:A calendar system classification that is used by institutions that enroll students at any time during the academic year. For example, a cosmetology school or a word processing school might allow students to enroll and begin studies at various times, with no requirement that classes begin on a certain date.
51:Cooperative housing: | 51:Cooperative education program: | 64:College-owned, -operated, or -affiliated housing in which students share food and housing expenses and participate in household chores to reduce living expenses. | 71:A program that provides for alternate class attendance and employment in business, industry, or government.
51:*Counseling service: | 51:Credit: | 51:Credit course: | 56:Recognition of attendance or performance in an instructional activity (course or program) that can be applied by a recipient toward the requirements for a degree, diploma, certificate, or recognized postsecondary credential. | 60:A course that, if successfully completed, can be applied toward the number of courses required for achieving a degree, diploma, certificate, or other recognized postsecondary credential. | 64:Activities designed to assist students in making plans and decisions related to their education, career, or personal development.
51:Credit hour: | 51:Cross-registration: | 59:A unit of measure representing an hour (50 minutes) of instruction over a 15-week period in a semester or trimester system or a 10-week period in a quarter system. It is applied toward the total number of hours needed for completing the requirements of a degree, diploma, certificate, or recognized postsecondary credential. | 63:A system whereby students enrolled at one institution may take courses at another institution without having to apply to the second institution.
51:Deferred admission: | 51:Degree: | 56:An award conferred by a college, university, or other postsecondary education institution as official recognition for the successful completion of a program of studies. | 64:The practice of permitting admitted students to postpone enrollment, usually for a period of one academic term or one year.
51:*Developmental services: | 51:Degree-seeking students: | 67:Instructional courses designed for students deficient in the general competencies necessary for a regular postsecondary curriculum and educational setting. | 67:Students enrolled in courses for credit who are recognized by the institution as seeking a degree or recognized postsecondary credential. At the undergraduate level, this is intended to include students enrolled in vocational or occupational programs.
51:Differs by program (calendar system): | 51:Diploma: | 57:See | 59:Postsecondary award, certificate, or diploma. | 75:A calendar system classification that is used by institutions that have occupational/vocational programs of varying length. These schools may enroll students at specific times depending on the program desired. For example, a school might offer a two-month program in January, M arch, M ay, September, and November; and a three-month program in January, April, and October.
51:Distance learning: | 51:Doctor’s degree-research/scholarship | 63:An option for earning course credit at off-campus locations via cable television, internet, satellite classes, videotapes, correspondence courses, or other means. | 74:: A Ph.D. or other doctor's degree that requires advanced work beyond the master’s level, including the preparation and defense of a dissertation based on original research, or the planning and execution of an original project demonstrating substantial artistic or scholarly achievement. Some examples of this type of degree may include Ed.D., D.M .A., D.B.A., D.Sc., D.A., or D.M , and others, as designated by the awarding institution.
51:Doctor’s degree-professional practice | 51:Doctor’s degree-other | 51:Double major: | 60:Program in which students may complete two undergraduate programs of study simultaneously. | 64:: A doctor’s degree that does not meet the definition of a doctor’s degree - research/scholarship or a doctor’s degree - professional practice. | 74:: A doctor’s degree that is conferred upon completion of a program providing the knowledge and skills for the recognition, credential, or license required for professional practice. The degree is awarded after a period of study such that the total time to the degree, including both pre-professional and professional preparation, equals at least six full-time equivalent academic years. Some of these degrees were formerly classified as “first-professional” and may include: Chiropractic (D.C. or D.C.M .); Dentistry (D.D.S. or D.M .D.); Law (L.L.B. or J.D.); M edicine (M .D.); Optometry (O.D.); Osteopathic M edicine (D.O); Pharmacy (Pharm.D.); Podiatry (D.P.M ., Pod.D., D.P.); or, Veterinary M edicine (D.V.M .), and others, as designated by the awarding institution.
51:Dual enrollment: | 51:Early action plan: | 62:Refers to high school students enrolled in college courses for credit. In accordance with IPEDS, student performance is recorded on a college transcript and postsecondary credit is awarded for a passing grade in the course. Dual enrollment includes: All postsecondary courses, independent of course delivery mode, course location, course instructor, whether secondary credit is also offered, and whether the student enrolls through a formal state/local program or enrolls outside a formal state/local program. Dual enrollment excludes: Credit-by-exam models such as Advanced Placement (AP) and International Baccalaureate (IB) in which the student is not enrolled in a postsecondary institution. | 62:An admission plan that allows students to apply and be notified of an admission decision well in advance of the regular notification dates. If admitted, the candidate is not committed to enroll; the student may reply to the offer under the college’s regular reply policy.
51:Early admission: | 51:Early decision plan: | 62:A policy under which students who have not completed high school are admitted and enroll full time in college, usually after completion of their junior year. | 63:A plan that permits students to apply and be notified of an admission decision (and financial aid offer if applicable) well in advance of the regular notification date. Applicants agree to accept an offer of admission and, if admitted, to withdraw their applications from other colleges. There are three possible decisions for early decision applicants: admitted, denied, or not admitted but forwarded for consideration with the regular applicant pool, without prejudice.

51:External degree program: | 51:Extracurricular activities (as admission factor): | 67:A program of study in which students earn credits toward a degree through independent study, college courses, proficiency examinations, and personal experience. External degree programs require minimal or no classroom attendance. | 81:Special consideration in the admissions process given for participation in both school and nonschool-related activities of interest to the college, such as clubs, hobbies, student government, athletics, performing arts, etc.
51:First-time student: | 51:First-time, first-year student: | 63:A student attending any institution for the first time at the level enrolled. Includes students enrolled in the fall term who attended a postsecondary institution for the first time at the same level in the prior summer term. Also includes students who entered with advanced standing (college credit earned before graduation from high school). | 69:A student attending any institution for the first time at the undergraduate level. Includes students enrolled in the fall term who attended college for the first time in the prior summer term. Also includes students who entered with advanced standing (college credits earned before graduation from high school).
51:Full-time student (undergraduate): | 51:First-year student: | 51:Food and housing (charges)—on campus: | 63:A student who has completed less than the equivalent of 1 full year of undergraduate work; that is, less than 30 semester hours (in a 120-hour degree program) or less than 900 clock hours. | 73:A student enrolled for 12 or more semester credits, 12 or more quarter credits, or 24 or more clock hours a week each term. | 77:Assume double occupancy in institutional housing and 19 meals per week (or maximum meal plan).
51:Geographical residence (as admission factor): | 51:Grade-point average (academic high school GPA): | 80:Special consideration in the admission process given to students from a particular region, state, or country of residence. | 82:The sum of grade points a student has earned in secondary school divided by the number of courses taken. The most common system of assigning numbers to grades counts four points for an A, three points for a B, two points for a C, one point for a D, and no points for an E or F. Unweighted GPA’s assign the same weight to each course. Weighting gives students additional points for their grades in advanced or honors courses.

These include both independent nonprofit schools and those affiliated with a religious organization.

Credentials that are awarded to recognize an individual’s attainment of measurable technical or industry/occupational skills necessary to obtain employment or advance within an industry occupation. (Generally based on standards developed or endorsed by employers or industry associations).

Some schools, such as vocational and beauty schools, have year-round classes with no separate summer session. | 73:Special consideration given to students with demonstrated talent/abilities in areas of interest to the institution (e.g., sports, the arts, languages, etc.).

83:Financial Aid Definitions
51:External scholarships and grants: | 72:Scholarships and grants received from outside (private) sources that students bring with them (e.g., Kiwanis, National M erit scholarships). The institution may process paperwork to receive the dollars, but it has no role in determining the recipient or the dollar amount awarded.
51:Financial aid applicant: | 66:Any applicant who submits | 82:any one of | 89:the institutionally required financial aid applications/forms, such as the FAFSA.
51:Indebtedness: | 51:Institutional scholarships and grants | 60:Aggregate dollar amount borrowed through any loan program (federal, state, subsidized, unsubsidized, private, etc.; excluding parent loans) while the student was enrolled at an institution. Student loans co-signed by a parent are assumed to be the responsibility of the student and | 74:: Endowed scholarships, annual gifts and tuition funded grants for which the institution determines the recipient. | 220:should | 225:be included.
51:Financial need: | 61:As determined by your institution using the federal methodology and/or your institution's own standards.
51:Need-based aid: | 61:College-funded or college-administered award from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify. This includes both institutional and non-institutional student aid (grants, jobs, and loans).
51:Need-based scholarship or grant aid: | 74:Scholarships and grants from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify.
51:Need-based self-help aid: | 67:Loans and jobs from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must demonstrate financial need to qualify.
51:Note: S uggested order of precedence for counting non-need money as need-based: | 51:Non-need-based scholarship or grant aid: | 77:Scholarships and grants, gifts, or merit-based aid from institutional, state, federal, or other sources (including unrestricted funds or gifts and endowment income) awarded solely on the basis of academic achievement, merit, or any other non-need-based reason. When reporting questions H1 and H2, non-need-based aid that is used to meet need should be counted as need-based aid.

51:Work study and employment | 69:: Federal and state work study aid, and any employment packaged by your institution in financial aid awards.
